% wave-21 review repair: state the actual resident memory, including resolved
% minima maps and the hard-prime tuple, rather than only unresolved state.
The block's persistent state is linear in the hard-prime count: it keeps the
hard-prime tuple, unresolved sets, per-prime minimum maps, and a small core
cache.  It keeps only one bounded residue set for the current norm.  Prime
generation is chunked; no prime-by-slice factor table or interval-sized
residue array is retained.

\subsection{The remaining window}
The exact logical frontier and the finite plotting window can be displayed
together only if their labels remain separate:
\begingroup
\small
\begin{equation}\label{eq:remaining-window}\tag{56.16}
 \underbrace{A<1}_{\substack{\text{impossible pointwise}\\
                    \text{Theorems~\ref{thm:W-lower},
                    \ref{thm:slice-lower}}}}
 \quad\big|\quad
 \underbrace{1\leq A\ \lesssim\ 2}_{\substack{
        \text{\bfseries Assessment: finite-search window only}\\
        \log p,\ \log p\log\log p,\ (\log p)^2
        \text{ are the census anchors}}}
 \quad\big|\quad
 \underbrace{A\geq1}_{\substack{H_{\rm MOD}(A),H_{\rm SPF}(A)\\
                                  \text{still open pointwise}.}}
\end{equation}
\endgroup
The symbol $\lesssim2$ in the middle is deliberately not a proved upper
bound, a conjectured limiting exponent, or even a monotonic trend.  It only
names the normalization panel in (56.12) and (56.15).  The proved lower wall
is $A=1$; no finite census supplies the other side.  The pointwise-frontier
statements in Sections~\ref{sec:witness-tail} and~\ref{sec:lower-tail}
remain unchanged: Theorems~\ref{thm:typeII-certificate} and
\ref{thm:typeI-certificate} constrain only the complete-class certificate
architecture.

\section{The supercritical window and count-below-one reduction}
\label{sec:supercritical-window}
\medskip\noindent\textbf{Status.}
The count-below-one implication below is \textbf{proved conditional} on
explicit, falsifiable window hypotheses, which are \textsc{unproved}.  Its
fixed-polylogarithmic use inherits the provisional external-review
qualification on the Section~\ref{sec:cfree} moment/Bonferroni machinery;
its cubic use additionally inherits the
\textsc{claimed}/\allowbreak\textsc{provisional}
Theorem~\ref{thm:pruned}.  The only currently realized common power window
is $\theta=1/4$ in Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-tail}, with exactly those
qualifications, and it is below both thresholds.  Proposition~\ref{prop:mass-gap}
is proved only inside its explicitly defined absolute-remainder
architecture.  Statements that every sieve or linear combination belongs to
that architecture are Assessments, not theorems.  The crossing inputs are
open hypotheses.  Nothing here gives unconditional pointwise progress on
the Erd\H{o}s--Straus conjecture.

Put $L=\log N$, $t=\log T$, and
\[
 \mathcal E=\{p:p\text{ is prime and }4/p\text{ has no three-unit-fraction
 representation}\}.
\]
For every finite $T$ there is the load-bearing containment
\begin{equation}\tag{57.1}
 \mathcal E\subseteq\{p:W(p)=+\infty\}
       \subseteq\{p:W(p)>T\}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, finite $W(p)$ supplies minimizing data (51.2), and
Lemma~\ref{lem:multiplier} gives the explicit representation
\eqref{eq:mult-id}.  That lemma has no primality, parity, or hard-class
hypothesis.  Thus every prime carrying a datum is covered, including one
also soluble by an easy identity; no classification is assumed.  The
empty-datum convention $W=+\infty$ in (51.1) is essential.

\subsection{Count below one}
The endpoint constant cannot be suppressed in the literal hypotheses.

\begin{hypothesis}[$H_{\rm WIN}(\theta;b)$; cubic window; open]
\label{hyp:WIN-cubic}
For some fixed $b,c,C>0$ and $N_0$, uniformly for $N\geq N_0$ and
$3\leq T\leq\exp\{bL^\theta\}$,
\begin{equation}\tag{57.2}
 \#\{p\leq N:p\text{ prime},\ W(p)>T\}
       \leq CN\exp\{-c(\log T)^3\}.
\end{equation}
The effective version requires all four constants to be effective.
\end{hypothesis}

\begin{hypothesis}[$H'_{\rm WIN}(\theta;b)$; square-log window; open]
\label{hyp:WIN-square}
With the same quantifier pattern and some fixed positive constants,
\begin{equation}\tag{57.3}
 \#\{p\leq N:p\text{ prime},\ W(p)>T\}
 \leq CN\exp\{-c(\log T)^2\log(2+\log T)\}.
\end{equation}
Using $\log\log T$ is asymptotically equivalent; the displayed form is
positive throughout the stated range and matches (51.13).  Write
$H_{\rm WIN}(\theta)$ or $H'_{\rm WIN}(\theta)$ when the corresponding
assertion holds for some $b>0$.
\end{hypothesis}

\begin{theorem}[count-below-one; proved conditional; source 57.1]
\label{thm:count-below-one}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=\arabic*.]
\item If $H_{\rm WIN}(\theta)$ holds with $\theta>1/3$, every sufficiently
large prime satisfies the Erd\H{o}s--Straus conjecture.
\item If $H'_{\rm WIN}(\theta)$ holds with $\theta\geq1/2$, the same
conclusion follows.  In particular, $\theta=1/2$ itself suffices; no
$+\epsilon$ is needed.
\end{enumerate}
The threshold is effective if the constants in the relevant hypothesis are
effective.  At the cubic endpoint $\theta=1/3$, $cb^3>1$ is sufficient.
If $cb^3=1$, the right side is the constant $C$, so it forces zero exactly
when $C<1$; if $cb^3<1$, it grows.  Without a favorable endpoint-constant
condition, one honestly needs $\theta>1/3$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Assume first $H_{\rm WIN}(\theta;b)$.  Fixed constants $b,c,C>0$ and $N_0$
work for every $N\geq N_0$ and every
$3\leq T\leq\exp\{b(\log N)^\theta\}$.  For such $N$, choose the single
admissible endpoint $T_N=\exp\{bL^\theta\}$ once it is at least 3.  The
logarithm of the right side of (57.2) is
\begin{equation}\tag{57.4}
                    \log C+L-cb^3L^{3\theta}.
\end{equation}
For $\theta>1/3$ this is negative for every sufficiently large $L$, after
accounting for both $L$ and $\log C$.  Hence the nonnegative integer
$A_N=\#\{p\leq N:p\text{ prime},\ W(p)>T_N\}$ is less than one and is zero.
At $\theta=1/3$, (57.4) is $\log C+(1-cb^3)L$, giving exactly the three
endpoint cases in the statement.

For $H'_{\rm WIN}(\theta;b)$ the same fixed-constant and endpoint-choice
quantifiers give logarithm
\begin{equation}\tag{57.5}
 \log C+L-cb^2L^{2\theta}\log(2+bL^\theta).
\end{equation}
It tends to $-\infty$ relative to $L$ for $\theta>1/2$.  At
$\theta=1/2$, its negative term is
$(cb^2/2+o(1))L\log L$, which dominates $L+\log C$ for every fixed
$b,c>0$.  For $\theta<1/2$ the saving is $o(L)$, so this inequality alone
cannot force the count below one.

Finally, let $p$ be a counterexample prime in a sufficiently large dyadic
range and choose dyadic $N$ with $p\leq N<2p$.  The definition (51.1) and
Lemma~\ref{lem:multiplier} give
\[
 p\in\mathcal E\Longrightarrow W(p)=+\infty
 \Longrightarrow W(p)>T_N\Longrightarrow p\text{ is counted by }A_N.
\]
The first implication holds for every counterexample prime and the second
for every finite $T_N$, not only for this endpoint.  But $A_N=0$.
Successive dyadic $N$ cover every sufficiently large prime.  Every threshold
is computable when $b,c,C,N_0$ are.
\end{proof}

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Calibration against the present
internally proved/provisional window.}
Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-tail} supplies both tails only in the common power
window
\begin{equation}\tag{57.6}
                         t\leq bL^{1/4}
\end{equation}
for sufficiently small effective $b$; the cubic row is
\textsc{claimed/provisional}, while the square-log row has only the
Section~\ref{sec:cfree} machinery-review qualification.  This is a window
exponent, not $H_{\rm WIN}(1/4;1)$: the endpoint constant matters.  The
square-log row is stronger than (57.6), since (51.12) permits
\begin{equation}\tag{57.7}
 t^3\log(2+t)\ll L,
       \qquad t\ll(L/\log L)^{1/3}.
\end{equation}
Thus it already has power-window exponent $1/3$, up to the displayed
$\log L=\log\log N$ factor, but its count crosses one only when
$t^2\log t\gg L$, at scale $(L/\log L)^{1/2}$.  The cubic row is valid at
$t^4\ll L$ and crosses at $t^3\gg L$.  Both realized rows pay exactly one
extra factor $t$ beyond supply mass.  This calibrates a conditional
reduction; it is not an unconditional Erd\H{o}s--Straus result.

\subsection{The mass-invariant gap}
The antecedents are not new here.  The proof of \eqref{eq:mult-mass} records
the prime-slice supply wall and its summed Bombieri--Vinogradov multiplicity
(source \S18.2, Assessment 18.3(b)).  Hypothesis~\ref{hyp:PF} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:conditional} are the paper's earlier mass/product-tail
budget and cubic optimization; source \S18.3 sharpens that budget to
$\min(K,J\log K)+Jt\lesssim L$ in (18.15), and Assessment 18.4 performs
both its square-log and cubic optimizations.  Lemma~\ref{lem:residue-transfer}
and Corollary~\ref{cor:monomial-transfer} are the paper's corrected form of
source Lemma 33.3 and its conditional finite-transfer ledger (33.6).  Most
directly, the actual Section~\ref{sec:witness-tail} assembly has
\begin{equation}\tag{57.8}
 \deg=O(\mu),\qquad \log d_{\rm term}=O(\mu t),\qquad
 \log\sum|c_{\rm term}|=O(\mu t)
\end{equation}
by \eqref{eq:witness-ledger}, source (51.11).  The next definition isolates
only the arithmetic common to those antecedents.

\begin{definition}[absolute-remainder mass-level architecture]
At depth $T=\e^{O(t)}$, an ${\rm AR}(\mu,t)$ argument has a nonnegative
majorant $\nu=\sum_Sc_S1_{a_S\ (d_S)}$ for the relevant void or conspiracy
set, uses truncation degree $J\asymp\mu$, and has CRT mean at most
$\e^{-c_0\mu}$.  It transfers to $[1,N]$ using each class evaluation only
through
\begin{equation}\tag{57.9}
 \#\{n\leq N:n\equiv a_S\pmod {d_S}\}={N\over d_S}+O(1)
\end{equation}
and the triangle inequality over the coefficient ledger.  The architecture
books level and absolute-remainder cost $\e^{O(Jt)}$ and declares the clean
main-term estimate valid only in the standard window
\begin{equation}\tag{57.10}
                              L\geq C_0\mu t.
\end{equation}
This formalizes one proof architecture; it does not assert that every linear
combination has this ledger or must be evaluated absolutely.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[mass-invariant gap; proved arithmetic in the
${\rm AR}$ architecture; source Proposition 57.2]
\label{prop:mass-gap}
Suppose the supply mass is $\mu=\mu(t)$ and an ${\rm AR}(\mu,t)$ argument
gives a tail $\ll N\e^{-c_0\mu}$ in (57.10).
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=\arabic*.]
\item If the implicit tail constant is $A>0$, the exact requirement from
this upper bound is $c_0\mu>L+\log A$.  A convenient fixed-margin
\emph{sufficient} condition is
\begin{equation}\tag{57.11}
                        c_0\mu\geq(1+\epsilon)L
\end{equation}
for some fixed $\epsilon>0$.  Either form implies $\mu\gg L$ and is
incompatible with (57.10) as $t\to\infty$.
\item If $\mu(t)\asymp t^a$ for fixed $a>0$, the largest saving exponent
certified in the validity window is
\begin{equation}\tag{57.12}
 \mu\ll L^{a/(a+1)},\qquad \theta_{\rm AR}={a\over a+1}<1.
\end{equation}
Thus increasing the polynomial supply mass alone never reaches a pointwise
conclusion in this architecture.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The exact count-below-one inequality implies $\mu\gg L$ for large $L$;
with (57.10), this gives $L\gg\mu t\gg Lt$, impossible for unbounded $t$.
If $\mu\asymp t^a$, then (57.10) says $t^{a+1}\ll L$; substitution proves
(57.12).
\end{proof}
For orientation, restoring the slowly varying $\log t$, $a=2$ is the
square-log $2/3$ scale of source (18.16); $a=3$ is the cubic $3/4$ scale of
Assessment 18.4 and Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-tail}.  The exponent changes
with mass, but the one-factor gap does not.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Proved device check (Bonferroni--CRT).}
The Sections~\ref{sec:cfree} and~\ref{sec:witness-tail} construction fits
the formal architecture as an implemented certificate: it uses
$J\asymp\mu$, and (51.11) gives the booked costs $\e^{O(\mu t)}$.  This is
only a ledger statement; the tail retains the labels in this section's
status paragraph.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Assessment 57.3 (two further standard devices,
and the general moral).}
In the standard growing-dimension fundamental-lemma/beta-sieve model, a void
saving $\e^{-c\mu}$ requires truncation order proportional to dimension or
mass, while its sieve level is a product of that many modulus scales,
$D=\e^{\Theta(\mu t)}$.  Its usual parameter condition
$s=\log D/t\gtrsim\mu$ reproduces (57.10).  This assesses that named
uniform-in-dimension implementation; it is not a no-go theorem for all
hypergraph or arithmetic sieves.

The product-modulus Montgomery large sieve has the same arithmetic.  A
product of $J$ modulus scales of entropy $t$ has
$Q=\e^{\Theta(Jt)}$; the useful range $Q^2\leq N$ gives
\begin{equation}\tag{57.13}
                              J\leq {L\over2t}.
\end{equation}
In the usual truncated-exponential model, the available partial saving is
\begin{equation}\tag{57.14}
 F(J)=J\log{\e\mu\over J},\qquad
 J\leq\min(\mu,L/(2t)).
\end{equation}
For $\mu=t^a$, put $t_0=(L/2)^{1/(a+1)}$.  If $t\leq t_0$, the maximum is
$F(\mu)=\mu=O(L^{a/(a+1)})$.  If $t=t_0u$, $u>1$, the cap gives
\begin{equation}\tag{57.15}
 F\leq {t_0^a\over u}\log(\e u^{a+1})=O_a(t_0^a),
\end{equation}
since the last factor is maximized at $u=\e^{a/(a+1)}$.  Thus this modeled
saving is $O_a(L^{a/(a+1)})=o(L)$ and cannot make the count less than one.
The calculus is proved; the Assessment is that this product-modulus
large-sieve implementation models a proposed assembly.  None of these three
checks excludes a sparse formula, signed cancellation, or input seeing the
actual remainder locations.  In particular, the broader claim ``no
absolute-remainder assembly can ever work'' is an \textbf{Assessment}, not
Proposition~\ref{prop:mass-gap}.

\subsection{Where the crossing lives}
For the $c$-free atoms, every compatible set of $r$ distinct atoms has
distinct prime coordinates by Lemma~\ref{lem:cfree}, each in
$(X^{1/2},X]$.  Hence
\begin{equation}\tag{57.16}
 d_S\geq\prod_{A\in S}\ell_A\geq X^{r/2},
       \qquad \log d_S\geq {rt\over2}.
\end{equation}
In the witness-tail polynomial, the matching upper bound follows from
$k\ell=\e^{O(t)}$.  At void-detecting degree $r\asymp\mu$, near crossing
$\mu\asymp L$, top-degree moduli satisfy
$d_S=\e^{\Theta(\mu t)}\gg N$.  A nonempty class modulo such $d_S$ contains
zero or one integer of $[1,N]$, and its $O(1)$ remainder is no longer a
small perturbation of $N/d_S$.

For a finite congruence expansion define
\begin{equation}\tag{57.17}
 \operatorname {Rem}_\nu(N)=\sum_Sc_S\left(
 \#\{n\leq N:n\equiv a_S\pmod {d_S}\}-{N\over d_S}\right).
\end{equation}
Then exactly
\begin{equation}\tag{57.18}
 \sum_{n\leq N}\nu(n)=N\mathbb E_{\rm CRT}\nu+
                         \operatorname {Rem}_\nu(N).
\end{equation}
Below crossing, Section~\ref{sec:witness-tail} bounds the remainder by
$\sum|c_S|$.  \textbf{Assessment (crossing model).}  Above crossing, the
missing information is cancellation among signed zero-or-one remainders
across an exponentially large subledger, modeled at void scale as
$\e^{\Theta(\mu)}$ selected classes.  This is an upper-transfer mirror of
source Lemma 33.3, which transfers a positive CRT lower bound only while its
absolute ledger is affordable.

Fix in advance a family $\mathscr F$ of pairs $(N,T)$ with $N,T\to\infty$
and a specified mass $\mu(T)\to\infty$.

\begin{hypothesis}[$H_{\rm XW}(\mathscr F,\mu)$; supercritical remainder
cancellation; open]\label{hyp:XW}
There are fixed $c_1,\epsilon,A>0$ and specified functions
$\eta(T)\geq0$, $\eta(T)\to0$, and $h(T)\geq0$, $h(T)=o(\mu(T))$, such that
for every sufficiently large $(N,T)\in\mathscr F$, writing $L=\log N$, if
\[
 \mu(T)\leq AL,\qquad c_1\mu(T)\geq(1+\epsilon)L,
\]
there is a finite nonnegative congruence expansion $\nu_{N,T}$, at least one
on every prime $\e^{h(T)}<p\leq N$ with $W(p)>T$, for which
\begin{equation}\tag{57.19}
 \mathbb E_{\rm CRT}\nu_{N,T}\leq\e^{-c_1\mu(T)},\qquad
 \operatorname {Rem}_{\nu_{N,T}}(N)
 \leq N\e^{-c_1\mu(T)+\eta(T)\mu(T)}.
\end{equation}
All constants, the family, and both error functions are quantified before
$(N,T)$.  The remainder bound is deliberately one-sided; absolute
cancellation would be stronger than needed.
\end{hypothesis}

\begin{proposition}[crossing implication; proved conditional; source
Proposition 57.4]\label{prop:crossing}
Under $H_{\rm XW}(\mathscr F,\mu)$, every sufficiently large covered pair
meeting the two crossing conditions has no prime
$\e^{h(T)}<p\leq N$ with $W(p)>T$.  If $\mathscr F$ contains such a pair
for every sufficiently large dyadic $N$, the Erd\H{o}s--Straus conjecture
holds for all sufficiently large primes.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Equations (57.18)--(57.19) give the honest two-term bound
\[
 \sum_{n\leq N}\nu_{N,T}(n)
 \leq2N\e^{-c_1\mu(T)+\eta(T)\mu(T)}.
\]
Since $\mu(T)\leq AL$, its logarithm is at most
$\log2-\epsilon L+\eta(T)AL<0$ eventually.  The majorant excludes every
stated tail prime.  Under dyadic coverage, the two crossing inequalities
give $\mu\asymp L$, hence $h=o(L)$ and $\e^h<N/2$ eventually.  Every large
prime lies in some $(N/2,N]$, and (57.1) excludes a counterexample.
\end{proof}

Alternatively, let $\mathcal C_T$ be the union of exact CRT classes avoiding
\emph{all} Lemma~\ref{lem:multiplier} witness classes of modulus at most
$T$, and let $\delta_T$ be its density in the finite CRT product.  Thus
$W(n)>T$ implies $n\in\mathcal C_T$.

\begin{hypothesis}[$H_{\rm EQ}(\mathscr F,\mu)$; fair-share conspiracy
classes; open]\label{hyp:EQ}
For a fixed family and mass, there are fixed $c_2,\epsilon,A>0$ and a
specified $\eta(T)\geq0$, $\eta(T)\to0$, such that for every sufficiently
large $(N,T)\in\mathscr F$ satisfying $\mu(T)\leq A\log N$,
$c_2\mu(T)\geq(1+\epsilon)\log N$, and
$\delta_T\leq\e^{-c_2\mu(T)}$,
\begin{equation}\tag{57.20}
 \#\{p\leq N:p\text{ prime},\ p\in\mathcal C_T\}
 \leq\e^{\eta(T)\mu(T)}\delta_T\pi(N).
\end{equation}
\end{hypothesis}
This is falsifiable once $\mathscr F,\mu,\eta$ are specified.  Its right
side is at most $\exp\{-\epsilon L+\eta(T)AL\}<1$ eventually, so it gives no
tail prime at each covered pair and, with dyadic coverage, the same
large-prime conclusion as Proposition~\ref{prop:crossing}.  Neither crossing
hypothesis is asserted.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Assessment (localization of the missing input).}
The residue-dispersion target (37.27), reduced to the short-cofactor endpoint
\eqref{eq:endpoint-core}, is an earlier pair-level request for cancellation
across arithmetic residue fibres.  Both remain \textsc{open}; even proving
them would not itself give the all-degree cancellation in (57.19).  They
nevertheless locate the same missing information: mass and absolute ledgers
do not control where compatible residues land.  The
all-avoider cylinder wall, Theorem~\ref{thm:cylinder} (source Theorem 33.1),
is consistent only at this diagnostic level.  It concerns the complete
intrinsic avoider, not every $c$-free family or every possible supercritical
assembly.

The lower-tail primes of Section~\ref{sec:lower-tail} do not contradict the
proposed crossing.  For the least prime $p_T\equiv1\pmod {M(T)}$,
Theorem~\ref{thm:W-lower} gives
\begin{equation}\tag{57.21}
 \begin{gathered}
 T+o(T)\leq\log p_T\leq(5.2+o(1))T,
 \qquad\log T=\log\log p_T+O(1),\\
 \mu(T)\asymp(\log\log p_T)^3=o(\log p_T).
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The last mass is the cubic calibration with its inherited status; the
square-log mass is smaller,
$\asymp(\log\log p_T)^2\log\log\log p_T$.  These residue-one escapes are
subcritical, not counterexamples to either crossing hypothesis.

\subsection{Finite arithmetic replay}
\medskip\noindent\textbf{Computational 57.6 (exact stated range;
informational).}
Verifier block \texttt{(bd)} computes with exact rational arithmetic
\begin{equation}\tag{57.22}
 m(T)=\sum_{\substack{M\leq T\\M\equiv3\ (4)}}{\omega(M)\over M},
 \qquad
 \omega(M)=\#\{-4D\pmod M:D\mid((M+1)/4)^2\},
\end{equation}
using the cached complete-harvest helper from block \texttt{(bc)}, and
performs a chunked scan through $3\cdot10^5$.  The displayed mass decimals
come from exact fractions.  The tails include all positive integers or all
primes:
\begingroup
\tiny
\setlength{\arraycolsep}{2pt}
\begin{equation}\tag{57.23}
\begin{array}{c|r|r|r|r|r}
T&m(T)&\#\{n\leq3\cdot10^5:W(n)>T\}&
 \#\{p\leq3\cdot10^5:W(p)>T\}&
 \rho_{\rm int}/\e^{-m}&\rho_{\rm prime}/\e^{-m}\\ \hline
100 &4.248031290751&5516&76&1.28647&0.20454\\
300 &7.349236274282&1064&5&5.51510&0.29907\\
1000&12.171587320339&569&0&366.47489&0\\
3000&18.126592298585&550&0&136621.93932&0
\end{array}
\end{equation}
\endgroup
The density denominators are $300000$ and the exact $25997$ primes.  These
ratios are \textsc{info}, not estimates or growth laws.  The next section
explains almost all high-$T$ integer excess: all 547 positive squares at
most 300000 have $W=+\infty$, accounting for 547 of 569 survivors at
$T=1000$ and 547 of 550 at $T=3000$.  Conversely, the isolated
$(p,W)=(2031121,2495)$ in (56.11) warns that a larger prime box may contain
an escape far above a naive small-box fair-share picture.  Neither finite
observation decides (57.19) or (57.20).

Block \texttt{(bd)} also replays (57.4)--(57.5) at
$L=10^3,10^4,10^5,10^6$ with constants normalized to one, including all
three $C$-cases at the cubic endpoint.  Its additional asymptotic,
fixed-margin surrogates are $t^3/(2L)$ and $t^2\log t/(2L)$ at
$t=L^\theta$.  In that normalization, cubic $\theta=0.40$ crosses at
$L=32$, while $\theta=0.25$ decreases, and square-log $\theta=1/2$ crosses
at $L=\e^4$.  These are surrogate crossings only; $L=\e^4$ is not an exact
crossing for $\log(2+t)$ in (57.5).  This arithmetic verifies neither window
hypothesis.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Computational 57.7 (exact toy only).}
For every distinct harvested atom through $X=15,23,31$, block \texttt{(bd)}
enumerates compatible sets and counts exact lcms above
$N_{\rm toy}=X^2$:
\begin{equation}\tag{57.24}
\begin{array}{c|r|r|r|r}
X&r&\#\text{ atoms}&\#\text{ compatible}&\#\{d_S>X^2\}\\ \hline
15&2&11&41&0\\15&3&11&57&45\\15&4&11&18&18\\
23&2&23&200&0\\23&3&23&846&765\\23&4&23&1809&1809\\
31&2&31&393&0\\31&3&31&2653&2418\\31&4&31&10389&10371
\end{array}
\end{equation}
This is a finite structural illustration of (57.16), not asymptotic
evidence.

A sequence violating (57.2) or (57.3) in its moving window falsifies that
hypothesis.  For fixed $(\mathscr F,\mu)$, universal failure of all permitted
candidates in (57.19), or of every allowed fixed-constant/error-function
choice in (57.20), would falsify the corresponding crossing hypothesis; one
bad expansion does not falsify existential $H_{\rm XW}$.  Proposition
\ref{prop:mass-gap} excludes neither a sparse identity, nonabsolute transfer,
nor a new distribution theorem, and the finite tables support neither
hypothesis.  The proved content is the conditional count-below-one
implication, the one-factor arithmetic gap in the stated architecture, and
the exact remainder identity (57.18).  There is no universal localization
theorem and no unconditional pointwise Erd\H{o}s--Straus claim.

\section{The inverse census and the integer/prime dichotomy}
\label{sec:inverse-census}
\medskip\noindent\textbf{Status and headline.}
The definitions, inverse equivalences, Jacobi argument, and bounds below are
proved.  The censuses are \textbf{Computational} and exact only on their
stated grids.  The Iwaniec statement was checked in full secondary sources
archived under \texttt{sources/jacobsthal-literature/}; the publisher did
not deliver the 1978 primary PDF, so no primary-source verification is
claimed.  The main structural fact is stronger than a generic Jacobsthal
estimate: the full harvested system misses every square, so
$W(m^2)=+\infty$ for every positive integer $m$.  The useful all-integer
inverse is therefore near-linear, while finding a prime survivor remains
open below the exponential congruence construction.  No finite table below
suggests a growth law.

\subsection{Moving inverses and an exact finite census}
The literal least-positive-integer definition is corrupted at once:
\begin{equation}\tag{58.1}
 W(1)=W(4)=+\infty,\qquad W(2)=3,\qquad W(3)=7.
\end{equation}
Thus $\min\{n\geq1:W(n)>T\}=1$ for every $T$.  A fixed prime with infinite
$W$, if one existed, would similarly corrupt a raw prime inverse without
bearing on eventual pointwise behavior.  Throughout this section use the
\textbf{moving convention}
\begin{equation}\tag{58.2}
 \begin{split}
 L_{\rm int}(T)&=\min\{n>T:W(n)>T\},\\
 L_p(T)&=\min\{p>T:p\text{ prime},\ W(p)>T\},\\
 L_h(T)&=\min\{p>T:p\equiv1\pmod {24},\ p\text{ prime},\ W(p)>T\}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
The minima are finite by Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape}.  The hard-prime
inverse $L_h$ is included because (56.10)--(56.11) use that class; it must
not be substituted for the all-prime inverse $L_p$.  For example,
\begin{equation*}
                     L_p(335)=22621,
       \qquad L_h(335)=1853329.
\end{equation*}

Lemma~\ref{lem:exact-R} makes the inverse condition exact:
\begin{equation}\tag{58.3}
 W(n)>T\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 n\not\equiv-4D\pmod M
 \quad\left(\begin{array}{l}M\leq T,\ M\equiv3\pmod4,\\
                         D\mid((M+1)/4)^2
       \end{array}\right).
\end{equation}
This ranges over the original data, not a selected subfamily.  Data (51.2)
with $M=k\ell$ and $uvw=(M+1)/4$ give $D=u^2w$ and
$-uv^{-1}\equiv-4D\pmod M$; conversely, the prime-by-prime construction in
Lemma~\ref{lem:exact-R} recovers positive $u,v,w$ for every such $D$, while
$k=1,\ell=M$ is allowed.  Thus no factorization, composite modulus, or
duplicated class is lost.  To certify an inverse row it is enough to harvest
all moduli through that row's threshold; no claim about a later or
nonexistent witness is used.

informational).}
Verifier block \texttt{(be)} harvests (58.3), streams integers and primes

\subsection{Square escape and shifted constraints}
\begin{theorem}[square escape and two inverse scales; proved; source
Theorem 58.1]\label{thm:square-escape}
For every positive integer $m$, $W(m^2)=+\infty$.  Consequently, for every
real $T\geq3$,
\begin{equation}\tag{58.8}
 T<L_{\rm int}(T)
 \leq(\lfloor\sqrt T\rfloor+1)^2
 \leq T+2\sqrt T+1,
\end{equation}
and also
\begin{equation}\tag{58.9}
 L_{\rm int}(T)\leq M(T)+1=\exp\{(1+o(1))T\}.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\tag{58.10}
 L_{\rm int}(T)\leq L_p(T)
 \leq\exp\{(5.2+o(1))T\}.
\end{equation}
The last upper bound also holds for $L_h(T)$.  All asymptotic upper bounds
are effective in the same sense as Theorem~\ref{thm:W-lower}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
We replay the full square quantifier chain.  Fix arbitrary $M=4A-1$ and
$D\mid A^2$.  Since $(A,M)=1$, also $(D,M)=1$; thus the apparently
degenerate case in which a prime dividing $M$ also divides $D$ is
impossible.  A factor 2 in $D$ causes no common factor because $M$ is odd.
Hence every class $-4D\pmod M$ is a unit.

For every odd prime $r\mid A$, quadratic reciprocity,
$M\equiv-1\pmod r$, and $M\equiv3\pmod4$ give
\[
 \left(\frac rM\right)
 =\left(\frac Mr\right)(-1)^{(r-1)(M-1)/4}
 =\left(\frac{-1}r\right)(-1)^{(r-1)/2}=1.
\]
If $2\mid A$, then $M\equiv7\pmod8$, so $(2/M)=1$.
Multiplicativity therefore gives $(D/M)=1$ for every divisor $D\mid A^2$,
whether or not $D$ is a square and with every possible 2-adic exponent.
Since $(-1/M)=-1$ and $(4/M)=1$, one has $(-4D/M)=-1$.

For a local reading, write $M=\prod q^{e_q}$.  The last Jacobi sign says
that for at least one $q$ with odd $e_q$, $(-4D/q)=-1$; hence this class is
not a square modulo $q$, and therefore not modulo $q^{e_q}$ or $M$.  One
must not insist that this obstructing $q$ is itself $3\pmod4$: for
$(M,D)=(15,2)$, the class is a square modulo 3 and a nonsquare modulo 5.
The condition $M\equiv3\pmod4$ forces the \emph{global} Jacobi sign.
Finally, a nonunit square cannot be congruent to the unit $-4D$.  Thus every
square avoids every class for every eligible $M,D$; (58.3) gives
$W(m^2)=+\infty$.

The strict lower bound in (58.8) is definitional, not a claim that all
integers at most $T$ were witnessed.  Taking
$s=\lfloor\sqrt T\rfloor+1$ gives the upper bounds.

For (58.9), $M(T)+1\equiv1\pmod m$ for every eligible $m\leq T$.  If its
datum had $uvw=(m+1)/4$, the witness congruence would give $m\mid u+v$,
whereas
\[
 2\leq u+v\leq uv+1\leq(m+1)/4+1<m.
\]
This is the arithmetic part of Theorem~\ref{thm:W-lower} and uses no
primality.  Finally, primes are a subset of the integers, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:W-lower} supplies a prime in $1\pmod {M(T)}$ below
$\exp\{(5.2+o(1))T\}$.  For $T\geq8$ that class is also
$1\pmod {24}$, proving the hard-prime assertion.
\end{proof}

No lower bound $L_{\rm int}(T)\gg T\log T$, much less a superpolynomial one,
is possible under (58.2).  The following elementary shifts explain why a
naive lower-bound search can still look tempting.

\begin{lemma}[two necessary shifted-sieve conditions; proved; source Lemma
58.2]\label{lem:shifted-sieve}
If $W(n)>T$, then
\begin{equation}\tag{58.11}
 \begin{split}
 \ell\nmid n+4&\quad(\ell\leq T,\ \ell\equiv3\pmod4,
                         \ \ell\text{ prime}),\\
 \ell\nmid n+8&\quad(\ell\leq T,\ \ell\equiv7\pmod8,
                         \ \ell\text{ prime}).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For the first line choose $D=1$ in (58.3).  For a prime
$\ell\equiv3\pmod4$, put $A=(\ell+1)/4$.  The divisor $D=2$ is eligible
exactly when $2\mid A^2$, equivalently $A$ is even, equivalently
$\ell\equiv7\pmod8$.  Its class is $-8\pmod\ell$.
\end{proof}
The first line says that every $3\pmod4$ prime factor of $n+4$ exceeds $T$.
If $T\geq x+4$, the $n\leq x$ passing this one test are shifts of numbers
supported on 2 and primes $1\pmod4$.  The contextual Selberg--Delange
calculation has order $x/\sqrt{\log x}$, from the Dirichlet series
\begin{equation}\tag{58.12}
 \prod_{\substack{q=2\ \mathrm{or}\ q\equiv1\ (4)}}
 (1-q^{-s})^{-1}=\zeta(s)^{1/2}H(s),
\end{equation}
where $H$ is holomorphic and positive at 1.  This classical calculation is
not used as a new theorem.  It already shows why one shift cannot force a
superpolynomial first survivor.  The full conjunction is stronger, but
Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape} gives an exact common escape.

\subsection{What Jacobsthal does, and what it does not do}
For $q>0$, let $g(q)$ be the least $G$ such that every $G$ consecutive
integers contain one coprime to $q$; up to the conventional endpoint
difference of one, this is the maximal gap between consecutive integers
coprime to $q$.

\begin{theorem}[Iwaniec's Jacobsthal bound; secondary-source-verified;
source Theorem 58.3]\label{thm:jacobsthal}
If $k=\omega(q)\geq2$, uniformly in $q$,
\begin{equation}\tag{58.13}
                              g(q)\ll(k\log k)^2.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $g(q)\ll(k\log(2k))^2$ for all $q>1$, including the
one-prime-factor case.  The constant is absolute but was not explicit in the
checked formulation.  Page 1 of the archived full Costello--Watts paper
\cite{CostelloWatts} calls this the best \emph{asymptotic} upper bound,
displays $g(n)\leq X(k\log k)^2$ with unknown $X$, and attributes it to
Iwaniec \cite{IwaniecJacobsthal}.  As an independent scope check, pages 2--3
of the archived Ford--Green--Konyagin--Maynard--Tao paper
\cite{FordGreenKonyaginMaynardTao} define the endpoint-shifted maximal-gap
function $j$, identify $Y(x)=j(\prod_{p\leq x}p)-1$, and record Iwaniec's
consequence $Y(x)\ll x^2$.  The primary DOI is
\href{https://doi.org/10.1515/dema-1978-0121}{10.1515/dema-1978-0121};
automated retrieval reached the publisher's human-verification page rather
than a PDF.  This is secondary-source verification, not inspection of the
1978 primary paper.
\end{theorem}

There is a precise one-way map to (58.3).  For $P_3(T)$ from (56.1), avoiding
only $D=1$ is exactly
\begin{equation}\tag{58.14}
                         (n+4,P_3(T))=1.
\end{equation}
Since $\omega(P_3(T))=\pi(T;4,3)$, (58.13) says every interval of length
$O(T^2)$ contains a survivor of this subsystem.  This does \emph{not}
upper-bound $L_{\rm int}(T)$: avoiding the full system is harder, so the
full survivor set is a subset of the set in (58.14).  Reversing this
inclusion is the Jacobsthal direction error.

The full system removes, for every composite as well as prime
$M\equiv3\pmod4$, the structured family
\begin{equation}\tag{58.15}
 \{-4D\pmod M:D\mid((M+1)/4)^2\}.
\end{equation}
This is not deletion of one class modulo each prime.  A composite class in
(58.15) is an intersection of coordinate conditions, not a union of fixed
forbidden classes at its prime factors, so Iwaniec's theorem does not apply
verbatim.  Ford--Konyagin--Maynard--Pomerance--Tao
\cite{FordKonyaginMaynardPomeranceTao} allow bounded sets $I_p$ at each
prime under a one-dimensional average hypothesis, but their theorem
constructs \emph{large gaps} in a prime-indexed sifted set; it neither bounds
the first survivor nor covers (58.15).  The same warning applies to the
lower bounds in \cite{FordGreenKonyaginMaynardTao}: they concern a maximal,
worst-position gap, not this specified least element.

For the full all-integer system the Jacobsthal comparison is only historical
context, and potentially context for prime- or nonsquare-restricted
variants.  Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape} instead proves
\begin{equation}\tag{58.16}
                         L_{\rm int}(T)=T+O(\sqrt T).
\end{equation}
This means exactly the two-sided interval (58.8), not an asymptotic constant
for the difference.

\begin{corollary}[integer/prime count-below-one dichotomy; proved; source
Corollary 58.4]\label{cor:integer-prime-dichotomy}
For all $N>T$,
\begin{equation}\tag{58.17}
 \begin{gathered}
 \#\{T<n\leq N:W(n)>T\}
 \geq\max\{0,\lfloor\sqrt N\rfloor-\lfloor\sqrt T\rfloor\},\\
 N_0:=L_{\rm int}(T)\leq S:=(\lfloor\sqrt T\rfloor+1)^2,\\
 S\leq T+2\sqrt T+1,\qquad W(S)=+\infty.
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The moving count excludes $n\leq T$, so its nonemptiness is not the vacuous
result of $W(1)=W(4)=+\infty$.  At $N=N_0$ the left side is at least one by
definition, and at $N=S$ the square is a structural survivor.  For fixed
$T$, or $T=o(N)$, the all-integer tail contains
$(1+o(1))\sqrt N$ squares.  Thus an all-integer moving-tail bound below one
cannot hold at $N=N_0$ or $N=S$.  Any count-below-one route to a pointwise
prime theorem must use primality essentially: $S$ is composite for every
$T\geq3$.
\end{corollary}
This dichotomy retroactively explains the prime restriction in
Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-tail}; an all-integer analogue could not support the
count-below-one step in Section~\ref{sec:supercritical-window}.

\subsection{Prime survivors: congruence wall and technology audit}
Squares settle the integer inverse but provide no prime.  Theorem
\ref{thm:typeII-certificate} says a complete congruence certificate for
$W>T$ has modulus divisible by $P_3(T)$ and therefore at least
$\exp\{(1/2+o(1))T\}$.  Coupling such a certificate to a generic least-prime
theorem remains an $\exp\{\Theta(T)\}$ architecture.  Residue one and
Linnik give (58.10).

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Assessment 58.1 (the missing prime technology).}
Density alone gives no least-prime theorem for an arbitrary sifted set, and
ordinary upper-bound sieves have the parity obstruction: they bound
survivors without producing a prime.  Bombieri--Vinogradov-type distribution
handles many small progression conditions on average, but no theorem found
in this audit produces a prime avoiding the complete $T$-dependent family
(58.15) below $\exp\{o(T)\}$.  Work on divisors or smoothness of one shifted
prime concerns sizes and locations of factors of $p+a$, not a least-prime
theorem for all $p+4D$ here.  In particular, the first condition in (58.11)
is not a substitute for the full harvest.

The Generalized Riemann Hypothesis improves least primes in a specified
progression but does not remove parity for this union of sifted classes.  A
Cram\'er-type independent-prime model would put a first prime near the
reciprocal of modeled survivor density, up to logarithmic factors.  That is
a \textbf{Heuristic}, not a conditional theorem here.  The only proved prime
bounds are
\begin{equation}\tag{58.18}
 T<L_{\rm int}(T)\leq L_p(T)
          \leq\exp\{(5.2+o(1))T\}.
\end{equation}
Beating the exponential upper end requires aggregate information about
primes in this sifted set, not merely one complete residue class.

\subsection{Exact inverse dualities and the $A$-window}
The slogan that superlogarithmic records are equivalent to
$L_p(T)=\exp\{o(T)\}$ needs a quantifier correction.

\begin{lemma}[record and pointwise inverse dualities; proved; source Lemma
58.5]\label{lem:inverse-dualities}
Under (58.2):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=\arabic*.]
\item There are primes $p_j\to\infty$ with
$W(p_j)/\log p_j\to\infty$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\tag{58.19}
             \liminf_{T\to\infty}{\log L_p(T)\over T}=0.
\end{equation}
The global statement $L_p(T)=\exp\{o(T)\}$, with a limit rather than a
subsequence, is sufficient but is not equivalent without regularity
controlling record gaps.
\item For every fixed $A>0$,
\begin{equation}\tag{58.20}
 H_{\rm MOD}(A)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 L_p((\log x)^A)>x\quad\text{for all sufficiently large }x.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $H_{\rm MOD}(A)$ fails exactly when
$L_p(T)\leq\exp(T^{1/A})$ for arbitrarily large $T$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Given a superlogarithmic sequence, choose
$T_j<\min(W(p_j),p_j)$ with $T_j/\log p_j\to\infty$; this is possible
because both $W(p_j)/\log p_j$ and $p_j/\log p_j$ diverge.  Then
$L_p(T_j)\leq p_j$, so
$0\leq\log L_p(T_j)/T_j\leq\log p_j/T_j\to0$.  Conversely, choose
$T_j\to\infty$ along the liminf and put $p_j=L_p(T_j)$.  Then
$p_j>T_j\to\infty$, $W(p_j)>T_j$, and
$W(p_j)/\log p_j>T_j/\log L_p(T_j)\to\infty$.  This includes
$W=+\infty$.

For (58.20), negate both sides.  If $H_{\rm MOD}(A)$ fails at arbitrarily
large primes $p$, put $x=p$.  Since $(\log p)^A<p$ eventually, that prime is
admissible and $L_p((\log x)^A)\leq x$.  Conversely, if this inverse
inequality holds at unbounded $x$, put
$q=L_p((\log x)^A)\leq x$.  Then $q\to\infty$ and
$W(q)>(\log x)^A\geq(\log q)^A$, so $H_{\rm MOD}(A)$ fails.  Substituting
$T=(\log x)^A$ gives failures
$L_p(T)\leq\exp(T^{1/A})$ at arbitrarily large $T$; conversely, any
unbounded such $T$ gives $x=\exp(T^{1/A})$ and a failure of the right side
of (58.20).
\end{proof}

Formula (58.20) is the exact inverse form of the remaining window.  For
$A<1$, Theorem~\ref{thm:W-lower} gives
$L_p(T)\leq\exp\{(5.2+o(1))T\}\ll\exp(T^{1/A})$, reproving failure of
$H_{\rm MOD}(A)$.  At $A=1$, the hypothesis asks for the eventual lower
bound $L_p(T)>\e^T$; for $A>1$, it asks for
$L_p(T)>\exp(T^{1/A})$.  Neither (58.10) nor the census decides any
$A\geq1$.  The $1\leq A\lesssim2$ display (56.16) remains a finite
normalization window, not a claimed exponent.

\section{Polynomial escape: complete through degree two, cyclotomic in general}
\label{sec:polynomial-escape}
\medskip\noindent\textbf{Status and stakes.}
The local reduction, the qualitative Chebotarev obstruction, the complete
degree-at-most-two classification, and the all-integer tail obstruction below
are \textbf{proved}.  The classification in degree greater than two remains
\textbf{Conjectural}; in particular, this section does not claim a full
polynomial classification.  The finite censuses are \textbf{Computational}
and exact only in their displayed ranges.  The algebraic results use
Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape}, qualitative Chebotarev, Schur's prime-divisor
theorem, and the standard conductor description of quadratic fields.  They
do not use Theorem~\ref{thm:pruned} or any of the Section~\ref{sec:cfree}
moment/Bonferroni machinery, and inherit none of their provisional status.
The Chebotarev obstruction is qualitative and \textbf{ineffective}: no
effective least prime is extracted.

The stakes are asymmetric.  The square family makes an all-integer cubic
tail impossible as soon as its saving passes the one-half-density crossing:
there are about $N^{1/2}$ structural survivors.  Thus the prime restriction
in Theorems~\ref{thm:witness-tail} and~\ref{thm:count-below-one} is necessary,
not cosmetic.  In the other direction, a uniform escape polynomial taking
prime values infinitely often would give infinitely many primes with
$W=+\infty$, permanently outside the Type-II frame and violating every
pointwise $H_{\rm MOD}(A)$.  A proof that every such polynomial is
square-valued would close this polynomial-family counterexample flank,
because a positive square greater than one is composite.
Theorem~\ref{thm:quadratic-escape} closes it only through degree two; the
higher-degree flank and nonpolynomial thin families remain open.

\subsection{Uniform escape and its exact local form}
For $D=\prod r^{e_r}$, put
\begin{equation}\tag{59.1}
             R(D)=\prod_r r^{\lceil e_r/2\rceil}.
\end{equation}
Thus $D\mid A^2$ if and only if $R(D)\mid A$; this is the same radical
with half-rounded exponents denoted $R_0(D)$ earlier.

\begin{definition}[uniform polynomial escape; source Definition 59.1]
\label{def:uniform-polynomial-escape}
Let $f\in\mathbb Z[X]$ have degree at least one and be eventually positive
on the positive integers.  It is a \emph{uniform polynomial escape} if there
is an $m_0$ such that
\begin{equation}\tag{59.2}
                       W(f(m))=+\infty\qquad(m\geq m_0).
\end{equation}
\end{definition}
The threshold permits the finitely many negative, zero, or exceptional
initial values and nothing else.  A polynomial identity $f=g^2$, with
$g\in\mathbb Z[X]$ nonconstant, is a uniform escape by
Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape}; take $m_0$ beyond the finitely many integral
zeros of $g$.  Constants are excluded.  A positive constant $c$ would
qualify exactly when $W(c)=+\infty$, so including degree zero would build the
open constant classification into the definition.  Known examples include
all positive square constants, in particular the anomalies $1$ and $4$ in
(58.1).

\begin{lemma}[exact local reduction; proved; source Lemma 59.1]
\label{lem:polynomial-local}
An eventually positive $f\in\mathbb Z[X]$ of positive degree is a uniform
polynomial escape if and only if, for every $M\equiv3\pmod4$ and every
$D\mid((M+1)/4)^2$,
\begin{equation}\tag{59.3}
                         f(x)\equiv-4D\pmod M
\end{equation}
has no solution $x\pmod M$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If (59.3) has a solution, its residue class contains infinitely many positive
integers $m$, including arbitrarily large ones beyond both the positivity and
escape thresholds.  Equation (58.3) then gives $W(f(m))\leq M$, a
contradiction.  Conversely, any finite witness for a tail value $f(m)$
supplies exactly one congruence (59.3) by the complete
original-data/divisor-form equivalence in (58.3).  Thus absence of every
local solution is equivalent to (59.2).
\end{proof}

There is a distinct progression variant.  Fix $a\pmod q$ and require (59.2)
only for sufficiently large $m\equiv a\pmod q$.  The corresponding criterion
is that (59.3) have no integral solution satisfying
\begin{equation}\tag{59.4}
                         x\equiv a\pmod{(M,q)}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, (59.4) is exactly the Chinese-remainder compatibility condition
between a solution modulo $M$ and the chosen class modulo $q$.  This variant
can admit more escape mechanisms and is not classified below.  It must also
be separated from \emph{finite-depth} escape: for fixed $T$, the choice
$q=\lcm(1,\ldots,T)$, $a=1$, and $f(X)=X$ gives the
Section~\ref{sec:lower-tail} class with $W(m)>T$.  It is not an
infinite-depth escape.  For any fixed $q$, a prime
$\ell\equiv3\pmod4$, $\ell\nmid q$, lets the Chinese remainder theorem
combine $m\equiv a\pmod q$ with $m\equiv-4\pmod\ell$, and the $D=1$ class
then hits that progression.

\subsection{The cyclotomic obstruction in every degree}
The $D=1$ subsystem already has a precise Galois meaning.  The other shifts
give a useful strengthened form of the same statement.

\begin{theorem}[root-field/cyclotomic obstruction; proved using qualitative
Chebotarev; ineffective; source Theorem 59.2]
\label{thm:cyclotomic-obstruction}
Fix $D\geq1$, put $Q_D=4R(D)$, and let
$C_D=\mathbb Q(\zeta_{Q_D})$.  Let $\tau_D$ be complex conjugation on
$C_D$.  For a root $\alpha$ of
\begin{equation}\tag{59.5}
                         h_D(X)=f(X)+4D,
\end{equation}
put $F_\alpha=\mathbb Q(\alpha)$.  Apart from the finite ramified and
leading-coefficient primes, the polynomial $h_D$ has no root modulo any
prime
\begin{equation}\tag{59.6}
                         p\equiv-1\pmod {Q_D}
\end{equation}
if and only if
\begin{equation}\tag{59.7}
 \tau_D|_{F_\alpha\cap C_D}\ne1
                 \quad\hbox{for every root }\alpha.
\end{equation}
Consequently every uniform polynomial escape satisfies (59.7) for every
$D$.  At $D=1$, this specializes to
\begin{equation}\tag{59.8}
              \mathbb Q(i)\subseteq\mathbb Q(\alpha)
       \quad\hbox{for every root of }f(X)+4.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The eligibility condition for a prime modulus is
\begin{equation}\tag{59.9}
 D\mid((p+1)/4)^2
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad R(D)\mid(p+1)/4
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad p\equiv-1\pmod {Q_D}.
\end{equation}
Suppose first that $\tau_D$ is trivial on $F_\alpha\cap C_D$.  The identity
on $F_\alpha$ and $\tau_D$ on $C_D$ then agree on the intersection, so they
glue on $F_\alpha C_D$.  Extend the resulting automorphism to a Galois
closure of $h_D$ composited with $C_D$.  It fixes $\alpha$ and restricts to
$\tau_D$ on the cyclotomic field.  Qualitative Chebotarev supplies
infinitely many unramified primes in its conjugacy class.  Their cyclotomic
Frobenius is $-1$, hence they satisfy (59.6), while their polynomial
Frobenius fixes a root, so $h_D$ has a root modulo each of them.

Conversely, a good prime in (59.6) at which $h_D$ has a root has a Frobenius
element restricting to $\tau_D$ and fixing some root.  Its restriction
forces $\tau_D$ to be trivial on that root field's intersection with $C_D$.
This proves the all-but-finitely-many equivalence.  Lemma~\ref{lem:polynomial-local}
and the prime data (59.9) give the necessary condition for a uniform escape.
When $D=1$, the cyclotomic field is $\mathbb Q(i)$; its intersection with a
root field is either $\mathbb Q$ or $\mathbb Q(i)$, and complex conjugation
is nontrivial only in the second case.  This is (59.8).
\end{proof}

No effective least prime is extracted here.  In permutation language, if
$K$ is the splitting field of $f+4$, the union of conjugacy classes in
${\rm Gal}(K/\mathbb Q)$ which fix a root lies inside
${\rm Gal}(K/\mathbb Q(i))$.  The root-field formulation (59.8), rather
than the looser statement merely that $K$ contains $i$, is the load-bearing
conclusion.  For example, the nonsquare polynomial $f=X^4-3$ passes the
$D=1$ test because $f+4=X^4+1$ has root field
$\mathbb Q(\zeta_8)$.  It is not a uniform escape:
$(M,D,x)=(7,2,2)$ solves (59.3).  Thus the other $D$'s really are needed.

\subsection{Complete classification through degree two}
\begin{theorem}[linear and quadratic uniform escapes; proved; source
Theorem 59.3]
\label{thm:quadratic-escape}
Let $f\in\mathbb Z[X]$ be eventually positive and $1\leq\deg f\leq2$.
Then $f$ is a uniform polynomial escape if and only if
\begin{equation}\tag{59.10}
                         f(X)=g(X)^2
\end{equation}
for some $g\in\mathbb Z[X]$.  In particular there is no linear uniform
escape.  The identity (59.10) is an identity of polynomials, not merely an
agreement after the threshold $m_0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $f$ is linear, $f+4$ has a root modulo every prime not dividing its
leading coefficient.  Infinitely many primes are $3\pmod4$, and only
finitely many divide that coefficient, so one of them contradicts the
eligible $D=1$ subsystem.  Thus no coprimality assumption on the leading
coefficient is hidden here.

Now write
\begin{equation}\tag{59.11}
                         f(X)=aX^2+bX+c.
\end{equation}
Eventual positivity gives $a>0$; thus the $a<0$ sign is excluded before any
character argument.  Theorem~\ref{thm:cyclotomic-obstruction} at $D=1$ says
that the root field of $f+4$ contains $\mathbb Q(i)$.  A reducible
quadratic, including the degenerate discriminant-zero case, has a rational
root field and is therefore impossible.  An irreducible quadratic has only
its own quadratic root field, so that field must equal $\mathbb Q(i)$.
This rules out every positive discriminant and every negative square class
other than that of $-1$.  Equivalently its integral discriminant is
\begin{equation}\tag{59.12}
              \Delta_1=b^2-4a(c+4)=-r^2
\end{equation}
for a nonzero integer $r$.

For a general fixed $D$, the discriminant of $f+4D$ is
\begin{equation}\tag{59.13}
 \Delta_D=-S_D,
 \qquad S_D=r^2+16a(D-1)=16aD+B,
 \qquad B=r^2-16a.
\end{equation}
Suppose $B\ne0$.  Choose an odd prime $q\nmid2aB$.  The linear congruence
$16aD+B\equiv0\pmod q$ has $q$ lifts modulo $q^2$, exactly one of which
also vanishes modulo $q^2$.  Choose a positive $D$ in one of the other
lifts.  Then
\begin{equation}\tag{59.14}
                q\parallel S_D,
                \qquad q\nmid D,
                \qquad q\nmid Q_D=4R(D).
\end{equation}
The quadratic field $\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-S_D})$ consequently has fundamental
conductor divisible by $q$, so it is not contained in
$\mathbb Q(\zeta_{Q_D})$.  Its intersection with that cyclotomic field is
therefore $\mathbb Q$.  Complex conjugation acts trivially on the
intersection, contradicting Theorem~\ref{thm:cyclotomic-obstruction}.
Hence $B=0$, so $r^2=16a$.  Write $r=4d$; then $a=d^2$.
Equation (59.12) now reduces to $b^2=4d^2c$.  Thus $b=2de$ and $c=e^2$
for an integer $e$, and
\begin{equation}\tag{59.15}
                         f(X)=(dX+e)^2.
\end{equation}
The divisibilities follow prime by prime from $4d^2\mid b^2$.  Conversely
every polynomial (59.15) escapes by Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape}.
\end{proof}

The proof shows why using only a short fixed list of shifts would be
misleading: the decisive $D$ is selected from the coefficients through a
new prime $q$.  Concrete nonsquare replays which pass the \emph{whole}
$D=1$ prime layer are
\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
f&\operatorname {disc}(f+4)&(M,D,x)\text{ hitting }f(x)+4D\\ \hline
2X^2+2X-3&-4&(11,3,3)\\
X^2-4X+1&-4&(7,2,5)\\
X^2-6X+30&-100&(11,3,3).
\end{array}
\end{equation*}
Here no prime $3\pmod4$ divides the displayed square root of the negative
discriminant.  Verifier block \texttt{(bf)} checks the $D=1$ assertion over
a finite prime range and each exact later-shift hit.  The theorem, not that
finite replay, proves that every nonsquare quadratic is eventually hit.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Conjecture $C_{\rm POLY}$ (full polynomial escape
classification).}
Every uniform polynomial escape in Definition~\ref{def:uniform-polynomial-escape}
is a square in $\mathbb Z[X]$.

This is a falsifiable higher-degree extension of
Theorem~\ref{thm:quadratic-escape}, not a theorem.
Theorem~\ref{thm:cyclotomic-obstruction} is only a necessary local structure
theorem there.  Root fields such as $\mathbb Q(\zeta_8)$ show that its
$D=1$ condition alone does not imply a square polynomial; pinning all shifts
(59.5) in arbitrary degree is the remaining algebraic problem.

\begin{corollary}[prime values in the classified range; proved; source
Corollary 59.4]
\label{cor:polynomial-primes}
No positive-degree uniform escape of degree at most two takes a positive
prime value.  Hence no linear or quadratic polynomial family can produce an
unbounded sequence of primes with $W=+\infty$.  Conditional on
$C_{\rm POLY}$, the same statement holds for every polynomial degree.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:quadratic-escape} every positive value is an integer
square.  It is then either $1$ or composite.  The conditional extension is
identical.
\end{proof}

This is the Type-II polynomial-level mirror of Corollary~\ref{cor:no-third-law}
only in a qualified sense.  Type I has no third fixed congruence-level
vanishing law; Type II does have the square polynomial law, now proved
unique through degree two.  Corollary~\ref{cor:polynomial-primes} closes the
polynomial-family route to permanent Type-II-less primes only in that
range.  Exponential sequences, recurrence orbits, arbitrary thin sets, the
progression variant (59.4), and all higher-degree polynomials are outside
its unconditional scope.

\subsection{The all-integer cubic tail fails at the square crossing}
\begin{proposition}[integer-tail obstruction; proved; source Proposition
59.5]
\label{prop:integer-tail-obstruction}
For every $N>T\geq1$,
\begin{equation}\tag{59.16}
 \#\{n:T<n\leq N,\ W(n)>T\}
 \geq \lfloor\sqrt N\rfloor-\lfloor\sqrt T\rfloor.
\end{equation}
Consequently an all-integer analogue of the cubic hypothesis (57.2),
\begin{equation}\tag{59.17}
 \#\{n:T<n\leq N,\ W(n)>T\}
       \leq C N\exp\{-c(\log T)^3\},
\end{equation}
cannot hold uniformly in any window containing
\begin{equation}\tag{59.18}
 \log T=\lambda(\log N)^{1/3}
 \quad\hbox{with}\quad \lambda>(1/(2c))^{1/3}.
\end{equation}
Any unshifted all-integer analogue also bounds the moving set in (59.17), so
the same contradiction applies.  In particular it fails in every power
window $\log T\leq b(\log N)^\theta$ with $b,c>0$ and $\theta>1/3$.
At $\theta=1/3$, the square obstruction contradicts (59.17) when
$cb^3>1/2$; equality leaves the prefactor and endpoint floors to decide.
The constant $(1/(2c))^{1/3}$ is the exact crossing for this
square-lower-bound comparison, not a claim that no other integer obstruction
acts earlier.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every square in $(T,N]$ has $W=+\infty$ by
Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape}, which proves (59.16).  Put $L=\log N$ and
$t=\log T$.  At $t=\lambda L^{1/3}$, one has $T=o(N)$, and the logarithms
of the square lower bound and the proposed upper bound are respectively
\begin{equation}\tag{59.19}
             {1\over2}L+o(L),
       \qquad \log C+L-c\lambda^3L.
\end{equation}
The upper bound is smaller when $c\lambda^3>1/2$, a contradiction.  If
$\theta>1/3$, choose any fixed
$\lambda>(1/(2c))^{1/3}$.  Then $t=\lambda L^{1/3}$ lies inside the window
$t\leq bL^\theta$ for all large $L$, and still has $T<N$; applying the
bound at that interior point gives the contradiction.
\end{proof}

Thus Section~\ref{sec:supercritical-window}'s count-below-one implication is
prime-essential before one even asks whether its open window hypothesis can
be proved.  This sharpens the integer/prime dichotomy (58.17): for the
supercritical extension needed there, the prime restriction in
Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-tail} is logically necessary, not cosmetic.  The
presently realized $\theta=1/4$ window and all its inherited status labels
are unchanged.

\subsection{The refuted complete-square proposal and finite census}
\medskip\noindent\textbf{Former Conjecture $C_{\rm SQ}$ (complete square
escape; refuted in Section~\ref{sec:witness-duality}).}
\begin{equation}\tag{59.20}
       \{n\in\mathbb Z_{>0}:W(n)=+\infty\}
             =\{m^2:m\in\mathbb Z_{>0}\}.
\end{equation}
Source Section~59 proposed (59.20), whose right side already includes $1$
and $4$.  The computer-assisted Theorem~\ref{thm:nonsquare-escapes} now
refutes it by proving three nonsquare escapes.  Its implication structure
remains useful: had (59.20) held, it would have been \emph{strictly
stronger} than Erd\H{o}s--Straus, because every prime is a nonsquare and a
finite Type-II witness yields an explicit three-unit-fraction representation
by Lemma~\ref{lem:multiplier}.  The reverse implication was never claimed:
Erd\H{o}s--Straus alone neither forces this witness type nor says anything
about composite nonsquares.  The former conjecture would also have implied
$C_{\rm POLY}$ by the following standard lemma.  The repaired open statement
$C_{\rm SQ}'$ appears in (60.22); $C_{\rm POLY}$ remains open.

\begin{lemma}[eventual square values force a square polynomial; proved;
source Lemma 59.6]
\label{lem:eventual-square-polynomial}
If $f\in\mathbb Z[X]$ is eventually positive and $f(n)$ is a square for all
sufficiently large integers $n$, then $f=g^2$ for some $g\in\mathbb Z[X]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Remove the square factors of $f$ in $\mathbb Q[X]$.  If the remaining
squarefree polynomial $H$ is nonconstant, Schur's theorem gives infinitely
many prime divisors of its values.  Choose one avoiding the leading
coefficient, discriminant, and the fixed rational content.  It has a simple
root $a\pmod p$.  Of the $p$ lifts of $a$ modulo $p^2$, exactly one makes
$p^2\mid H(n)$; any other lift contains arbitrarily large $n$ and makes the
valuation of $f(n)$ odd, a contradiction.  Thus the squarefree part is
constant.  That constant must be a rational square, because one nonzero
square value has its square class.  Hence $f$ is a square in
$\mathbb Q[X]$.  Gauss's lemma then makes its square root integral: writing
it as $(u/v)G$ with $(u,v)=1$ and primitive $G\in\mathbb Z[X]$, integrality
of $(u^2/v^2)G^2$ forces $v=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[twisted-square survivor reduction; proved; source Lemma 59.7]
\label{lem:twisted-square}
Put $n=sm^2$, where $s$ is squarefree.  If eligible $(M,D)$, with
$M=4A-1$ and $D\mid A^2$, hits $n$, then
\begin{equation}\tag{59.21}
 (M,sm)=1,\qquad \left(\frac{s}{M}\right)=-1,
 \qquad s\nmid D.
\end{equation}
Moreover, if $t\mid m$ and $t^2\mid D$, then the hit is equivalent to the
eligible auxiliary hit
\begin{equation}\tag{59.22}
 M\mid s(m/t)^2+4D/t^2.
\end{equation}
Conversely an auxiliary hit with divisor $E$ lifts to $sm^2$ through
$D=t^2E$ exactly when $t^2E\mid A^2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape} gives
$\left(\frac{D}{M}\right)=1$ for every eligible $D$, while
$\left(\frac{-1}{M}\right)=-1$.  A hit makes $-4D$, hence $n$, a unit
modulo $M$.  Taking Jacobi symbols in $sm^2\equiv-4D\pmod M$ gives the
middle assertion of (59.21).  If $D=sE$, then
$M\mid s(m^2+4E)$; cancelling the unit $s$ would make the square $m^2$ hit
the eligible divisor $E$, contrary to Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape}.
Finally
\[
 sm^2+4D=t^2\{s(m/t)^2+4D/t^2\}.
\]
Here $t$ is a unit modulo $M$, because $t^2\mid D$ and $(D,M)=1$.  This
proves both directions and the stated lift condition.
\end{proof}

For the three finite-census survivors, Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-square} makes
the historical searches exact and substantially thinner.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item For $288=2\cdot12^2$, one must have $M\equiv3\pmod8$,
$3\nmid M$, and $D$ odd, and the residual congruence is
$D\equiv-72\pmod M$.  Directly, if $D=2E$, then
$288+4D=2(12^2+4E)$, an impossible square hit; the redundant deeper layer
$D=8E$ gives $8(6^2+4E)$.  Thus no even-$D$ layer was left in the scan.
\item For $336=21\cdot4^2$, one must have $(M,21)=1$,
$\left(\frac M3\right)\left(\frac M7\right)=-1$,
$21\nmid D$, and $D\equiv-84\pmod M$.  Here quadratic reciprocity gives
$\left(\frac {21}M\right)=
 \left(\frac M3\right)\left(\frac M7\right)$.  If $D=21E$, then
$336+4D=21(4^2+4E)$, which is impossible.  The exact scaling chain is
$4\mid D\Rightarrow336\to84$ and $16\mid D\Rightarrow336\to21$.
Although $W(21)=11$ via $(M,E)=(11,3)$, lifting that datum would require
$16E=48\mid A^2=9$, which fails.
\item For $4545=505\cdot3^2$, one must have
$(M,3\cdot5\cdot101)=1$,
$\left(\frac M5\right)\left(\frac M{101}\right)=-1$, and
$505\nmid D$, with $D\equiv-4545A\pmod M$.  The forbidden layer is
$4545+4(505E)=505(3^2+4E)$.  If $9\mid D$, the datum scales down to one for
505.  The least such datum, again $(11,3)$, does not lift:
$9\cdot3\nmid3^2$.
\end{itemize}
These are necessary filters plus an exact replay of the residual divisor
congruence, not by themselves an infinitude proof.  In particular the
character-permitted moduli are nonempty, and the argument does not force a
nonsquare at one prime factor of every such $M$.  At the source-Section-59
stage these reductions therefore made no $W=+\infty$ claim; the additional
finite normal form in Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-duality} is what closes the
search in Theorem~\ref{thm:nonsquare-escapes}.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Computational 59.8 (exact finite scan; historical
$C_{\rm SQ}$ stress test).}
Verifier block \texttt{(bf)} replays the complete divisor harvest and, below
$3\cdot10^5$, removes the known squares from the tail.  The exact nonsquare
counts are
\begin{equation}\tag{59.23}
\begin{array}{c|rrrr}
T&100&300&1000&3000\\ \hline
\#\{n\leq3\cdot10^5:n\ne\square,\ W(n)>T\}
 &4969&517&22&3.
\end{array}
\end{equation}
The three nonsquare survivors in the last column, now proved by
Theorem~\ref{thm:nonsquare-escapes} to have $W=+\infty$, are
\begin{equation}\tag{59.24}
                         288,\qquad336,\qquad4545.
\end{equation}
The default independent smallest-prime-factor scan uses Python integers for
every divisor product and checks every eligible modulus through
$3\cdot10^6$.  At the Section-59 stage it proved only the finite statement
\begin{equation}\tag{59.25}
             W(288),W(336),W(4545)>3\cdot10^6.
\end{equation}
The wave-23 deep run checked all moduli through $1.5\cdot10^8$, after the
character filters above, and enumerated $2{,}878{,}826{,}874$ divisor values
without a hit.  Thus the stronger exact computational frontier at that stage was
\begin{equation}\tag{59.26}
             W(288),W(336),W(4545)>1.5\cdot10^8.
\end{equation}
The global ceilings in (60.19) now show retrospectively that this finite run
had crossed the whole witness space, while Theorem
\ref{thm:nonsquare-escapes} supplies the proof through the finite normal
form and an independent exhaustion.  The historical run used Python
integers throughout; it peaked below 170 MB.  The
modulus/divisor phase streams one value of $A$ at a time and retains no old
divisor list.  Previous external quick-probe values
$506063,754379,652271$, respectively, came from fixed-width integer overflow,
including impossible negative divisors.  They are false and are explicitly
rejected: they are not $W$-values.  Setting \texttt{ES\_FULL\_SCAN=1}
reproduces (59.26).  It also raises the population endpoint to $10^6$, where
the nonsquare counts at the same threshold grid are
$(17007,1841,77,4)$.  The fourth depth-3000 survivor is 643245 and has the
exact witness $W(643245)=3119$.

As a family control, block \texttt{(bf)} computes every least finite $W$ for
$1\leq m\leq60$ in the three natural twisted-square families.  The table
records histograms $\{W:\#m\}$; the single lower-bound entry in each row is
the former finite-census survivor in (59.24).
\begin{equation}\tag{59.27}
\begin{array}{c|l|c}
 n&\{W(n):\#m\},\quad1\leq m\leq60&\text{deep survivor}\\ \hline
 2m^2&\{3:40,11:11,19:4,35:1,59:2,131:1\}&m=12\\
 21m^2&\{11:33,19:9,23:15,31:1,71:1\}&m=4\\
 505m^2&\{11:33,23:23,47:1,87:1,107:1\}&m=3.
\end{array}
\end{equation}
Thus the factorization $n=sm^2$ is not by itself an escape mechanism:
177 of the 180 controls are hit by $M\leq131$.  The three exceptional
multipliers are highly atypical in this finite sample.  This observation is
not the proof of their escape; Theorem~\ref{thm:nonsquare-escapes} uses the
separate exhaustive normal-form certificate.  The same block checks
Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-square} exactly over a bounded complete harvest, pins
all 180 table entries, tests three nonsquare quadratics passing the $D=1$
layer, replays the discriminant-character dichotomy, and verifies the
coefficient-dependent construction (59.14).  None of these finite checks
substitutes for Chebotarev or for an unbounded classification.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Walls and relation to the frontier.}
The three values in (59.24) were the live finite stress test for
$C_{\rm SQ}$, not positive evidence for it.  Section
\ref{sec:witness-duality} now proves that all three have $W=+\infty$, refutes
$C_{\rm SQ}$, and replaces it by open $C_{\rm SQ}'$.  This does not alter
the degree-two classification or prove that there are no further nonsquare
escapes.  It also yields no unconditional pointwise prime conclusion.  In
higher polynomial degree, Theorem~\ref{thm:cyclotomic-obstruction} remains
only a necessary, qualitatively ineffective obstruction, and
$C_{\rm POLY}$ remains open.

\section{Witness duality, finite decidability, and the survivor frontier}
\label{sec:witness-duality}
\medskip\noindent\textbf{Status and correction to the proposed frontier.}
The dual criterion, its finite normal form, the bound on every possible
datum, and the partial swap symmetry below are proved.  The resolution of
the three Section~\ref{sec:polynomial-escape} survivors is a
\textbf{computer-assisted theorem}: the reduction is proved, and verifier
block \texttt{(bg)} exhausts its small exact integer certificate by two
parameterizations.  The displayed replay and near-miss tables are
\textbf{Computational}, exact only in their stated ranges.  No prime-tail or
Erd\H{o}s--Straus theorem follows.

The suggestion that fixed small $a$ can correspond to arbitrarily large
$M$ is false for fixed $n$.  Eligibility on the $M$-side forces a second
divisor condition on the $a$-side.  Together the two conditions put every
datum for fixed $n$ in a finite box.  This correction resolves, rather than
merely extends, the survivor frontier.

\subsection{Exact duality, cancellation, and the finite normal form}
Call $(M,D)$ a witness datum for $n$ when
\begin{equation}\tag{60.1}
 M\equiv3\pmod4,\qquad A={M+1\over4},\qquad D\mid A^2,
 \qquad M\mid n+4D.
\end{equation}
This is the complete harvested criterion (58.3), not a selected Type-II
subfamily.  It reparameterizes Lemma~\ref{lem:multiplier}'s intrinsic
divisor system; it is not a new Egyptian-fraction identity family.  The new
content is the dual cancellation ledger and finite normal form.

\begin{theorem}[witness duality and finite normal form; proved; source
Theorem 60.1]\label{thm:witness-duality}
Let $n\geq1$.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item A datum (60.1) exists if and only if there are positive integers
$a,D$ such that, on putting
\begin{equation}\tag{60.2}
 a\equiv-n\pmod4,\qquad h={n+a\over4},\qquad A={D+h\over a},
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}\tag{60.3}
 D\mid h^2,\qquad a\mid D+h,\qquad D\mid A^2.
\end{equation}
The two maps are
\begin{equation}\tag{60.4}
 a={n+4D\over M},\qquad M=4A-1={n+4D\over a},
\end{equation}
and are inverse.  Thus all quantities in (60.2)--(60.4) are positive
integers.

\item Once $D\mid h^2$ and $a\mid D+h$ hold, the exact cancellation ledger
is
\begin{equation}\tag{60.5}
 D\mid h^2\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 {D\over(D,a^2)}\mid A^2,\qquad h\equiv aA\pmod D.
\end{equation}
Hence the last check $D\mid A^2$ in (60.3) is redundant when $(a,D)=1$,
but not in general.  If $q\mid(a,D)$, then $q\mid n$.  More precisely, for
every prime $q$, with $d=v_q(D)$, $\alpha=v_q(a)$, and $\beta=v_q(A)$,
the dual condition certifies
\begin{equation}\tag{60.6}
 \max(d-2\alpha,0)\leq2\beta,
\end{equation}
whereas eligibility requires $d\leq2\beta$.  This includes $q=2$: if $D$
is even, no cancellation of the powers of $2$ in $a^2$ is free.

\item There is a canonical normal form involving no divisors of large
numbers.  Witness data are in bijection with positive integers $(g,u,v,d)$
satisfying
\begin{equation}\tag{60.7}
 (u,v)=1,\qquad d\mid g,\qquad a:=4gv-n\geq1,\qquad au=d+v,
\end{equation}
through
\begin{equation}\tag{60.8}
 A=gu,\qquad h=gv,\qquad D=gd,\qquad M=4gu-1.
\end{equation}
If $B=\lfloor(n+1)/3\rfloor$, every datum satisfies
\begin{equation}\tag{60.9}
 g,v\leq B,\qquad a\leq2B,\qquad u\leq2B,
 \qquad M\leq8B^2-1.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
From (60.1), define $a$ by (60.4).  Reduction modulo $4$ gives
$a\equiv-n\pmod4$.  Direct expansion, stronger than merely squaring a
congruence, gives
\begin{equation}\tag{60.10}
 aA={a(M+1)\over4}=D+{n+a\over4}=D+h.
\end{equation}
Thus $h\equiv aA\pmod D$, and $D\mid A^2$ implies $D\mid h^2$.
Conversely, (60.2)--(60.3) make $A$ integral and positive.  With $M=4A-1$,
equation (60.10) and $4h=n+a$ give $aM=n+4D$; the last condition in
(60.3) gives eligibility.  This proves the first equivalence.

Prime by prime, $D\mid a^2A^2$ says $d\leq2\alpha+2\beta$.  Removing from
$D$ the part already supplied by $a^2$ gives exactly (60.5)--(60.6).  Also
$(a,D)\mid aM-4D=n$.  This proves the cancellation claims, including the
even branch.

For the normal form, start with a datum and put
\begin{equation}\tag{60.11}
 g=(A,h),\qquad u=A/g,\qquad v=h/g.
\end{equation}
The divisor $D$ divides both $A^2$ and $h^2$, hence $D\mid g^2$.
Equation $D=aA-h$ also gives $g\mid D$.  Therefore $D=gd$ with $d\mid g$,
and division of (60.10) by $g$ gives $au=d+v$.  Conversely,
(60.7)--(60.8) give $D\mid g^2\mid A^2$ and (60.10), hence a witness.  The
coprimality in (60.7) makes the construction canonical.

Finally $au=d+v\leq g+v$, so $a\leq g+v$ and $u\leq g+v$.  Since
$a=4gv-n$,
\begin{equation}\tag{60.12}
 4gv\leq n+g+v.
\end{equation}
Using first $v\geq1$ and then $g\geq1$ in (60.12) gives $3g\leq n+1$ and
$3v\leq n+1$.  Thus $g,v\leq B$, and the remaining bounds in (60.9) follow
from $u,a\leq g+v$ and $M=4gu-1$.  No comparison or swap between $a$ and
$M$ is used.
\end{proof}

The factor $4$ in the seed congruence can therefore be removed without any
oddness assumption: (60.10) always gives the necessary condition
$D\mid((n+a)/4)^2$.  Oddness matters only when one tries to cancel $a$ and
infer eligibility from that condition.

For $n=288$, an eligible $D$ must be odd.  Indeed, if $D=2E$, then
$E\mid A^2$ and
\[
 M\mid288+4D=2(144+4E),
\]
so the odd $M$ would make the square $144=12^2$ hit the eligible divisor
$E$, contradicting Theorem~\ref{thm:square-escape}.  Lemma
\ref{lem:twisted-square} gives $M\equiv3\pmod8$, and the defining equation
then gives $a\equiv4\pmod8$.  Write $a=4r$, with $r$ odd.  Theorem
\ref{thm:witness-duality} becomes the exact criterion
\begin{equation}\tag{60.13}
 \begin{gathered}
 D\mid(72+r)^2,\qquad r\mid D+72,\qquad
 M={D+72\over r}\equiv3\pmod8,\\
 D\mid\left({M+1\over4}\right)^2.
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The last line is redundant if $(r,D)=1$.  In the noncoprime branch,
$(r,D)\mid72$, and oddness reduces this to $(r,D)\mid9$; a $3$-adic
eligibility check remains essential.  Bound (60.9) gives $a\leq192$, hence
only odd $r\leq47$.  The apparently unbounded quadratic sequence in
(60.13) is finite once both sides of the duality are enforced.

For $336$, write $a=4r$ and $h=84+r$.  The exact quotient conditions are
\begin{equation}\tag{60.13a}
 D\mid(84+r)^2,\quad r\mid D+84,\quad
 M=(D+84)/r\equiv3\pmod4,\quad
 D\mid((M+1)/4)^2,
\end{equation}
in addition to the proved necessary conditions $(M,21)=1$,
$(M/3)(M/7)=-1$, and $21\nmid D$.  Here $(a,D)\mid336$, so powers of $2$,
$3$, and $7$ are precisely the possible cancellation branches; (60.9)
gives $a\leq224$.  For $4545$, one has $a\equiv3\pmod4$,
$h=(4545+a)/4$, and the unshortened criterion (60.2)--(60.3), with
$(a,D)\mid4545=3^2\cdot5\cdot101$.  Its additional necessary filters are
$(M,4545)=1$, $(M/5)(M/101)=-1$, and $505\nmid D$, and $a\leq3030$.  Since
this last $a$ is odd, its $2$-adic dual condition already equals $2$-adic
eligibility; only primes $3$, $5$, and $101$ can be lost to cancellation.

\subsection{What swaps, and what does not}
The equation in (60.4) is symmetric in $a,M$, but its eligibility data are
not automatically symmetric.

\begin{lemma}[partial witness involution; proved; source Lemma 60.2]
\label{lem:witness-involution}
Let $(M,D,a)$ be a witness triple for $n$.  Swapping $M$ and $a$ gives
another witness triple for the same $n$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\tag{60.14}
 a\equiv3\pmod4,\qquad D\mid\left({a+1\over4}\right)^2.
\end{equation}
On this bi-eligible subset the map
\begin{equation}\tag{60.15}
 (M,D,a)\longmapsto(a,D,M)
\end{equation}
is a genuine involution, and $W(n)\leq\min(a,M)$.  Such a swap can occur
only when $n\equiv1\pmod4$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The swapped equation is the same equation.  Conditions (60.14) are exactly
the modulus class and harvested-divisor eligibility conditions for modulus
$a$.  Applying the swap twice is the identity.  Finally
$a\equiv-n\pmod4$, so $a\equiv3\pmod4$ forces $n\equiv1\pmod4$.
\end{proof}
For example, $(M,D,a)=(7,1,3)$ and $(3,1,7)$ are the paired data for
$n=17$.  The triple $(7,1,7)$ for $n=45$ is a fixed point.  There is no
universal involution even when $n\equiv1\pmod4$: $(23,36,7)$ is a datum for
$17$, but $36\nmid((7+1)/4)^2=4$.

Lemma~\ref{lem:multiplier} is the honest source of eligibility on each side:
the bi-eligible set is an intersection of two intrinsic harvested-divisor
systems.  It is not that lemma's divisor involution $D\mapsto A^2/D$, nor
the four-parameter Case-B dictionary: equation (60.4) merely records the
positive quotient of one intrinsic residue congruence.  Thus the raw
symmetry was latent in that congruence, while closure under the swap is the
additional condition (60.14), not a universal symmetry.

\subsection{The survivor frontier closes}
Before using the finite normal form, the suggested two-front scan has the
wrong geometry.  From $D\mid h^2$,
\begin{equation}\tag{60.16}
 M={n+4D\over a}\leq {a\over4}+{n\over2}+{n^2/4+n\over a}.
\end{equation}
From the original eligibility $D\leq((M+1)/4)^2$,
\begin{equation}\tag{60.17}
 a\leq {M\over4}+{1\over2}+{n+1/4\over M}.
\end{equation}
Consequently fixed small $a$ never sees arbitrarily large $M$ for fixed
$n$.  An $M$-scan through $M_0$ and an $a$-scan through $A_0$ cover exactly
\begin{equation}\tag{60.18}
 \{(M,D,a):M\leq M_0\ \hbox{or}\ a\leq A_0\};
\end{equation}
before using further structure; the uncovered region is exactly
$M>M_0,a>A_0$.  It is not a mysterious middle-divisor regime.  Theorem
\ref{thm:witness-duality} puts the whole witness space in (60.9), so
$A_0=2B$, or $M_0=8B^2-1$, leaves no uncovered region.

For the former finite-census survivors, the rigorous all-data bounds are
\begin{equation}\tag{60.19}
\begin{array}{c|r|r|r}
 n&B&a_{\max}=2B&M_{\max}=8B^2-1\\ \hline
 288&96&192&73{,}727\\
 336&112&224&100{,}351\\
 4545&1515&3030&18{,}361{,}799
\end{array}
\end{equation}
Thus even the historical exact $M\leq1.5\cdot10^8$ scan in (59.26) crossed
each now-proved global ceiling.  Block \texttt{(bg)} supplies a much smaller
independent certificate in the new coordinates.

\begin{theorem}[three nonsquare escapes; computer-assisted, proved; source
Theorem 60.3]\label{thm:nonsquare-escapes}
\begin{equation}\tag{60.20}
 W(288)=W(336)=W(4545)=+\infty.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-duality} proves that every possible datum occurs
with $1\leq a\leq2B$.  Block \texttt{(bg)} enumerates every admissible $a$
in those three finite intervals, every positive divisor $D\mid h^2$, and
checks both $a\mid D+h$ and the uncancelled eligibility $D\mid A^2$, using
Python integers.  It finds no eligible row.  Independently, the same block
enumerates every $(g,v)$ in (60.9), every $d\mid g$, and equation (60.7),
again finding no row.  These are exhaustive finite certificates, not
extrapolations from a cutoff.  Equivalence in Theorem
\ref{thm:witness-duality} proves (60.20).
\end{proof}
Here ``escape'' means escape from the harvested
Lemma~\ref{lem:multiplier} witness statistic $W$, not escape from
Egyptian-fraction solvability.  None of these composite integers is an
Erd\H{o}s--Straus counterexample; explicitly,
\begin{equation}\tag{60.20a}
 {4\over288}={1\over144}+{1\over216}+{1\over432},\qquad
 {4\over336}={1\over168}+{1\over252}+{1\over504},\qquad
 {4\over4545}={1\over1515}+{1\over6060}+{1\over18180}.
\end{equation}

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Computational 60.1 (exact survivor certificate
and near misses).}
Verifier block \texttt{(bg)} gives
\begin{equation}\tag{60.21}
\begin{array}{c|r|r|r|r|r|r}
 n&D\mid h^2&a\mid D+h&\text{\S59 necessary filters}&D\mid A^2
 &(g,v)\text{ pairs}&(g,v,d)\text{ tests}\\ \hline
288&756&43&4&0&66&209\\
336&912&65&37&0&78&262\\
4545&22{,}995&188&62&0&1{,}152&5{,}405
\end{array}
\end{equation}
The Section~\ref{sec:polynomial-escape} filter column applies, respectively,
the odd-$D$, $M\equiv3\pmod8$, and unit conditions; the $21$-Jacobi and
$21\nmid D$ conditions; and the $505$-Jacobi and $505\nmid D$ conditions.
Every row surviving that column has $(a,D)>1$, and every one fails exactly
the uncancelled prime-power eligibility check.  For $288$, all four have
$(a,D)=9$ and fail at $3$.  This is the gcd caveat in (60.5), not an
overflow artifact.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Assessment 60.1 (the failed character route).}
There is no global mod-$8$ obstruction at the proposed first layer: for
example, $D=1$ and $r\equiv3\pmod8$ already satisfy
$D\mid(72+r)^2$ and $D\equiv3r\pmod8$.  The complete conditions add the
quotient and uncancelled eligibility constraints.  Theorem
\ref{thm:witness-duality} makes their range finite, and (60.21) shows that
eligibility, not the Jacobi filters, is the final killer.  An asymptotic
expected-count argument over unbounded $r$ therefore addresses a
nonexistent frontier.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Heuristic 60.1 (uniform-residue comparison only).}
If the divisors in the first numerical column of (60.21) were uniform
modulo each $a$, the naive expected counts for $a\mid D+h$, namely
$\sum_a\tau(h^2)/a$, would be $13.266827,15.617351,49.909726$.  The observed
exact counts are $43,65,188$.  This mismatch is unsurprising for divisors of
a square; neither list predicts an eligible witness.  The comparison is a
finite diagnostic, not evidence for a tail law.

Conjecture $C_{\rm SQ}$ in (59.20) is therefore refuted.  The minimal repair
supported by current proofs is:

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Conjecture $C_{\rm SQ}'$ (repaired complete escape
set; open).}
\begin{equation}\tag{60.22}
 \{n\geq1:W(n)=+\infty\}
 =\{m^2:m\geq1\}\cup\{288,336,4545\}.
\end{equation}
Only the inclusion from right to left is proved.  No claim is made that the
three sporadics form a family or that there are no further nonsquare
escapes.  Since all three are composite, (60.22), if proved, would still
imply Erd\H{o}s--Straus.  It would also imply $C_{\rm POLY}$: an unbounded
polynomial cannot remain in the three-value sporadic set, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:eventual-square-polynomial} handles eventual square values.

\subsection{The statistic and the prime side}
Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-duality} gives an exact terminating algorithm for
$W(n)$.  Set $B=\lfloor(n+1)/3\rfloor$ and enumerate
\begin{equation}\tag{60.23}
 1\leq a\leq2B,\qquad a\equiv-n\pmod4,\qquad
 h=(n+a)/4,\qquad D\mid h^2.
\end{equation}
Retain $a\mid D+h$ and $D\mid((D+h)/a)^2$, and minimize
$M=4(D+h)/a-1$.  An empty list proves $W(n)=+\infty$.  Its divisor work is
exactly
\begin{equation}\tag{60.24}
 \sum_{\substack{a\leq2B\\a\equiv-n\ (4)}}
 \tau\!\left(((n+a)/4)^2\right),
\end{equation}
which has the usual average order $O(n(\log n)^2)$, apart from factoring
cost; in particular the corresponding per-prime decision at $n=p$ has
$O(p(\log p)^2)$ average divisor work.  A smallest-prime-factor sieve
suffices at moderate scale, while a segmented sieve keeps large censuses
memory-bounded.  By contrast, deciding from the original side using only
(60.9) scans $O(n^2)$ moduli and has divisor work of average order
$O(n^2(\log n)^2)$.  The $a$-algorithm is a genuine dimension reduction,
not an extension to large $M$ at fixed small $a$.

For an odd prime $p$, the gcd caveat disappears completely.  Indeed,
$(a,D)\mid p$, while (60.9) gives
$a\leq2\lfloor(p+1)/3\rfloor<p$.  Hence $(a,D)=1$, and (60.5) makes the
last eligibility check redundant.  We obtain the proved equivalence
\begin{equation}\tag{60.25}
\begin{split}
 W(p)<+\infty\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
